Let's analyze the naive PyTorch backward complexity and memory usage: - Forward: Q@K^T -> [B,H,N,N] (2BHN^2d FLOPs, BN^2H memory) - Softmax -> same memory - Attn@V -> [B,H,N,d] (2BHN^2d FLOPs) - Backward: - dV = Attn^T @ dO -> 2BHN^2d - dAttn = dO @ V^T -> 2BHN^2d - dScores = (dAttn - sum(dO*O, dim=-1, keepdim=True)) * Attn -> pointwise O(N^2) - dQ = dScores @ K -> 2BHN^2d - dK = dScores^T @ Q -> 2BHN^2d Total backward FLOPs: ~8BHN^2d = 8 * 2 * 32 * 1024^2 * 128 = 8 * 2 * 32 * 1,048,576 * 128 = let's calculate: 2*32 = 64, 64*1024^2 = 67,108,864, *128 = 8,589,934,592, *8 = 68,719,476,736 ≈ 68.7 GFLOPs. That's not huge. Naive PyTorch would create a 128MB attn matrix. Memory bandwidth for reading/writing it multiple times is significant. Our custom kernel should avoid creating the full N x N matrix in global memory if possible. We can recompute the attention scores on the fly for tiles. Since Br*Bc=8192, 256 threads need 32 steps to cover all. ``` for (int idx = tid; idx < Br*Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float sum = 0; for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) sum += float(Q[r][d]) * float(K[c][d]); S[r][c] = sum * scale; } ``` This is simple and should be fine. However, we need to be careful about performance: each thread computes a dot product of length 128, which is 128 FMAs. 32 steps * 128 FMAs = 4096 FMAs per thread. With 256 threads, total FLOPs = 1M per block. For 1024 blocks, total 1B FLOPs. At 67 TFLOPS (FP32) or 2 PFLOPS (BF16 TC), this is tiny. The real bottleneck is memory bandwidth. Since we are reading shared memory, it's fast. But writing P to global memory is 128MB, which at 3.35 TB/s is 0.04ms. So Kernel 1 time is dominated by global memory writes. That's fine. For computing O_i = P_ij @ V_j: ``` for (int idx = tid; idx < Br*D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; float sum = 0; for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) sum += float(P[r][c]) * float(V[c][d]); O_acc[r][d] += sum; } ``` Again, simple. Softmax: We need to compute m_i (max over j) and l_i (sum of exp). Pass 1: For each j tile, compute S and update m and l. ``` __shared__ float m[Br], l[Br]; if (tid < Br) { m[tid] = -INFINITY; l[tid] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); for each j tile: compute S __syncthreads(); for (int r = tid; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { float local_max = -INFINITY; for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) local_max = max(local_max, S[r][c]); // But wait, we need max over all j tiles. So: float new_m = max(m[r], local_max); float alpha = exp(m[r] - new_m); float local_sum = 0; for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) local_sum += exp(S[r][c] - new_m); l[r] = alpha * l[r] + local_sum; m[r] = new_m; } __syncthreads(); ``` After all j tiles, m[r] is the global max, l[r] is the global sum of exp(S - m). Pass 2: For each j tile, compute S again (or load from smem if we stored it? We can recompute to save smem or store to global memory scratch. But storing S for all j tiles is N*N. We could recompute S. Recomputing S is cheap. But we just computed K_j^T @ Q_i. If we recompute, we load K_j again. That's fine. Actually, we can store m and l after pass 1. Then in pass 2, we recompute S, compute P = exp(S - m) / l, write P to global, and accumulate O. But wait, V_j is needed for O, and K_j for S. In pass 2, we load K_j and V_j, compute S, then P, then accumulate O. However, we need m and l to be persistent across the two passes. We can store them in smem or registers. This is manageable. **Kernel 1b: compute_D** D = sum(dO * O, dim=-1). This is a simple reduction. Each block handles (b, hq, i_tile) of size Br. Load dO_i and O_i, compute dot product per row. Blocks: B * Hq * (N/Br) = 1024. **Kernel 2: compute_dQ** For each (b, hq, i_tile): Load Q_i, dO_i. For each j_tile: Load K_j, V_j. Compute dP_ij: dot(dO_i[row], V_j[col]). d=128. ``` for idx = tid; idx < Br*Bc; idx += blockDim.x: r = idx / Bc; c = idx % Bc; float dp = 0; for d=0..D-1: dp += float(dO[r][d]) * float(V[c][d]); dP[r][c] = dp; ``` Load D_i (Br floats) from global mem. Load P_ij from global mem. dS = (dP - D_i[:,None]) * P_ij. ``` for idx = tid; idx < Br*Bc; idx += blockDim.x: r = idx / Bc; c = idx % Bc; float ds = (dP[r][c] - D[r]) * P[r][c]; dS[r][c] = ds; ``` Then dQ_i += dS @ K_j. ``` for idx = tid; idx < Br*D; idx += blockDim.x: r = idx / D; d = idx % D; float sum = 0; for c=0..Bc-1: sum += dS[r][c] * float(K[c][d]); dQ_acc[r][d] += sum; ``` Write dQ_i to global mem (bf16). This kernel loads P_ij from global memory, K_j, V_j, Q_i, dO_i. It is memory-bound. Number of blocks: B*Hq*(N/Br) = 1024. **Kernel 3: compute_dK_dV** For each (b, kv_head, j_tile): Load K_j, V_j. Initialize dK_acc[Bc][D] = 0, dV_acc[Bc][D] = 0 as floats in smem or registers? Bc*D floats = 128*128*4 = 64KB. That fits in smem. For each q_head in the group (num_q_per_kv = 4): For each i_tile: Load Q_i, dO_i. Load P_ij from global mem. Compute dP_ij = dO_i @ V_j^T. Load D_i. Compute dS_ij = (dP_ij - D_i) * P_ij. Accumulate dK: for each c in Bc, for each d in D: dK[c][d] += sum_r dS[r][c] * Q[r][d]. Accumulate dV: for each c in Bc, for each d in D: dV[c][d] += sum_r P[r][c] * dO[r][d]. Write dK_j, dV_j to global mem. Number of blocks: B*Hkv*(N/Bc) = 2*8*(1024/128) = 2*8*8 = 128 blocks. That's too few! We need more parallelism. We need to split over d dimension as well. Let's think about the simplest custom approach that is likely correct and fast enough: store P, then compute dP, dS, dQ, dK, dV via a series of custom tile-based GEMM kernels. This is a lot of code but manageable. But writing custom GEMM kernels for (N x N) @ (N x D) with tiles is straightforward. Since N=1024, D=128, and we have many independent batched GEMMs. Let's define a generic batched GEMM kernel: `batched_gemm_nn` (A: [B, M, K], B: [B, K, N], C: [B, M, N]) For our case, each batched GEMM is B*Hq instances. Actually, we can write a kernel for `C = A @ B^T` and `C = A^T @ B`. But writing a high-performance generic GEMM is still hard. However, we can specialize to M=N=1024, K=128. **Specialized Kernel for dP = dO @ V^T** For each (b, hq, i, j): dP[b,hq,i,j] = sum_d dO[b,hq,i,d] * V[b,g,j,d] This is a batched matrix multiply with a shared V across groups. Actually V is [B, Hkv, N, D]. dO is [B, Hq, N, D]. dP is [B, Hq, N, N]. We can tile over i (Br) and j (Bc) and iterate over d (D=128). This is exactly a GEMM. We can write a kernel where each block computes a Br x Bc tile of dP. Load dO_i (Br x D) and V_j (Bc x D) into smem. Compute dot products over D. Since D=128 is small, we can load the whole D dimension into smem. This is a classic tile GEMM where the K dimension (D) is the inner dimension. Algorithm: ``` __shared__ float dO_s[Br][D]; // or bf16 __shared__ float V_s[Bc][D]; ``` For each (b, hq, i_tile, j_tile): Load dO_i into dO_s. Load V_j into V_s. __syncthreads(); for idx = tid; idx < Br*Bc; idx += blockDim.x: r = idx / Bc; c = idx % Bc; float sum = 0; for d=0..D-1: sum += dO_s[r][d] * V_s[c][d]; dP[b,hq,i_start+r,j_start+c] = sum; ``` This is simple and efficient because D is small (128). Shared memory: dO_s: Br * D * 2 bytes (bf16) = Br * 256 bytes. For Br=64, 16KB. V_s: Bc * D * 2 bytes. For Bc=64, 16KB. Total 32KB. Very low shared memory usage. We could increase Br and Bc. For Br=128, Bc=128, dO_s=32KB, V_s=32KB, total 64KB. Still fine. For Br=128, Bc=128, blocks = 2*32*8*8 = 4096 blocks. Each block has 256 threads. That's very good parallelism. For a given KV head `kv`, there are `num_q_per_kv` query heads: `q = kv * num_q_per_kv + qid`. The kernel block computes a Bc x D tile of dV for a given (b, kv, j_tile). So the plan is to implement these kernels in CUDA, load them via `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline`, and call them in sequence from Python. Let's verify the sizes and indices carefully. Input shapes: - q: [B, Hq, N, D] - k: [B, Hkv, N, D] - v: [B, Hkv, N, D] - dout: [B, Hq, N, D] Intermediates: - p: [B, Hq, N, N] (bf16) - o: [B, Hq, N, D] (bf16) - actually we might not need to store O if we compute D on the fly? Option C: Br=64, but use bf16 in smem with padding. For q_s: bf16 q_s[64][129] = 64*129*2 = 16.5KB. k_s: 16.5KB. v_s: 16.5KB. s_s: bf16 [64][65] = 8.3KB. o_acc: float [64][129] = 33KB. Total = 90.8KB. 2 blocks per SM, 16 warps, 25% occupancy. Better! And we avoid bank conflicts by padding bf16 arrays too. Wait, for bf16, bank conflicts occur because two bf16 elements per bank. With padding of 1 element per row: `__nv_bfloat16 q_s[Br][D+1]`. Then row_stride = (D+1)*2 = 258 bytes. 258/4 = 64.5, not integer? Actually bank index = (addr/2 % 32) or (addr/4 % 32)? For 16-bit accesses, two adjacent bf16 elements are in the same bank. So threads reading consecutive d from the same row will have 2-way conflicts. But in our dot product, threads read the same d? Let's see. In `compute_p_and_o`, when computing S: `for d = 0..D-1: sum += q_s[r][d] * k_s[c][d];` For a warp, threads have different (r,c) pairs. Some may have same r. When they read q_s[r][d], if multiple threads have the same r, it's a broadcast (no conflict). If they have different r, they read q_s[r1][d], q_s[r2][d], etc. With `q_s[Br][D+1]`, row_stride = (D+1)*2 = 258 bytes. 258 bytes offset between rows means bank difference = (258/2) % 32 = 129 % 32 = 1. So consecutive rows differ by 1 bank. So threads in a warp reading same d but different r have no conflicts (they read different banks). Good. For k_s, threads read k_s[c][d] with different c. Row_stride is also 258 bytes, so bank diff = 1 mod 32. No conflict. However, within a row, reading consecutive d: if a thread reads d and then d+1 in the loop, that's sequential, not across threads. So no conflict. What about loading into smem? When we load `q_s[r][d] = q[...]`, the threads loading should have consecutive threads mapping to consecutive memory addresses to have coalesced global loads. We can map `tid` to `r` and `d` with r as fastest or d as fastest. If we map `idx = tid` to `r = idx / D; d = idx % D`, then consecutive threads have consecutive d (varying fastest). In global memory, q is stored as [B,Hq,N,D] row-major, so consecutive d are contiguous. So load is coalesced. In shared memory, if we store as `q_s[r][d]` with d as fastest and padding, then consecutive threads write to consecutive d in the same row. For bf16, two threads per bank. So we have 2-way bank conflicts during the store. But stores to shared memory are fast, and 2-way conflict is acceptable. For loads in the dot product, threads access `q_s[r][d]` for varying r and fixed d across the loop. Since different r map to different banks (because row_stride%32=1), there is no conflict. Good. So using bf16 with `D+1` padding works well. Let's use bf16 for q_s, k_s, v_s, p_s, and float for accumulators (o_acc, m, l). Total smem for `compute_p_and_o` with Br=64: q_s: 64*129*2 = 16.5KB (bf16) k_s: 64*129*2 = 16.5KB v_s: 64*129*2 = 16.5KB p_s: 64*65*2 = 8.3KB o_acc: 64*129*4 = 33KB (float) m,l: 64*4*2 = 0.5KB Total = 91.3KB. 2 blocks per SM (182KB). 2 blocks * 8 warps = 16 warps per SM. Occupancy = 16/64 = 25%. Better. If we use threads=256 (8 warps). 2 blocks per SM -> 16 warps. We could also reduce threads to 128 (4 warps) to get 2 blocks * 4 = 8 warps (12.5%). That's worse. So 256 threads is fine. For `compute_ds_kernel`: dO_s: Br*(D+1)*2 = 16.5KB V_s: Bc*(D+1)*2 = 16.5KB P_s: Br*(Bc+1)*2 = 8.3KB D_s: Br*4 = 0.25KB Total = 41.5KB. Can have 5 blocks per SM. Blocks = 16384. Great occupancy. For `gemm_dQ_kernel`: dS_s: Br*(Bc+1)*4 = 64*65*4 = 16.6KB (float) K_s: Bc*(D+1)*4 = 64*129*4 = 33KB (float) dQ_acc: Br*(D+1)*4 = 33KB (float) Total = 82.6KB. 2 blocks per SM. Blocks=1024. Good. For `gemm_dV_kernel`: P_s: Br*(Bc+1)*2 = 8.3KB (bf16) dO_s: Br*(D+1)*2 = 16.5KB (bf16) dV_acc: Bc*(D+1)*4 = 33KB (float) Total = 57.8KB. 3 blocks per SM. Blocks = 256. Occupancy 3*8=24 warps? Wait block size for dV/dK kernels: we can use 256 threads (8 warps). But maybe we can use fewer. Let's see. If we use 256 threads, 3 blocks per SM -> 24 warps. Occupancy 24/64 = 37.5%. Good. Actually, for dV, we need to loop over qid and i_tile (64 iterations). Each iteration loads P and dO. So there is enough work. Similarly for `gemm_dK_kernel`: dS_s: 16.6KB (float) Q_s: 16.5KB (bf16) or float? Q_s is read many times. If we store as bf16, 16.5KB. If float, 33KB. Let's use bf16. dK_acc: 33KB (float) Total = 66KB. 3 blocks per SM. Wait, in `gemm_dK_kernel`, we need to compute dot product `Q[r][d]` with `dS[r][c]`. The access pattern: for fixed c and d, loop over r. Similar to gemm_dV, but Q replaces dO. So bf16 Q_s is fine. So the plan: - Use Br=64, Bc=64 for all kernels. - Use 256 threads per block. - Shared memory with padding to avoid bank conflicts. - Keep accumulators in float. Let's detail each kernel carefully. ### Kernel 1: `compute_p_and_o` Inputs: q, k, v. Outputs: p, o. Grid: (num_i_tiles, 1, B*Hq). Block: (256). num_i_tiles = N / Br = 1024/64 = 16. So grid.x=16, grid.y=1, grid.z=64 (B*Hq). ``` template<int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void compute_p_and_o_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* q, const __nv_bfloat16* k, const __nv_bfloat16* v, __nv_bfloat16* p, __nv_bfloat16* o, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, float scale) { // smem layout // q_s[Br][D+1] bf16 // k_s[Bc][D+1] bf16 // v_s[Bc][D+1] bf16 // p_s[Br][Bc+1] bf16 (for intermediate P) // o_acc[Br][D+1] float // m[Br], l[Br] float int b = blockIdx.z / Hq; int hq = blockIdx.z % Hq; int kv = hq / (Hq / Hkv); int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int i_start = i_tile * Br; extern __shared__ char smem[]; __nv_bfloat16* q_s = (__nv_bfloat16*)smem; __nv_bfloat16* k_s = q_s + Br * (D + 1); __nv_bfloat16* v_s = k_s + Bc * (D + 1); __nv_bfloat16* p_s = v_s + Bc * (D + 1); float* o_acc = (float*)(p_s + Br * (Bc + 1)); float* m = o_acc + Br * (D + 1); float* l = m + Br; // Load Q_i for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int q_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; q_s[r * (D + 1) + d] = q[q_idx]; } // Init m, l, o_acc for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { m[r] = -INFINITY; l[r] = 0.0f; } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { o_acc[idx] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); // Pass 1: compute m and l for (int j_tile = 0; j_tile < N / Bc; ++j_tile) { int j_start = j_tile * Bc; // Load K_j for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int k_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; k_s[c * (D + 1) + d] = k[k_idx]; } __syncthreads(); // Compute S for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += __bfloat162float(q_s[r * (D + 1) + d]) * __bfloat162float(k_s[c * (D + 1) + d]); } // store S temporarily in p_s as float? Wait p_s is bf16. We can avoid storing S. // Instead, we can store S in a float scratch. But we don't need to store it for pass 1. // Let's compute max and sum per thread for each r? No, the max needs to be reduced across c. // We can compute S, store in a float smem array, then do reduction. // We don't have a float smem for S. But we can reuse o_acc? No. // Let's store S in p_s by reinterpreting? p_s is bf16 array of size Br*(Bc+1). // For Br=64,Bc=64: 64*65 = 4160 elements. As float, that's 2080 floats (8KB). Not enough for 4096 floats (16KB). // We can allocate a separate float smem for S? That adds Br*(Bc+1)*4 ≈ 16KB. Total smem would be 91+16 = 107KB. Still okay. } } } ``` Hmm, we need a separate float smem for S in pass 1 and pass 2. Because to update m and l, we need to compute max over c and sum over c. If we compute S and immediately process it, we can avoid storing the whole tile. For example, each thread computes one row of S? But a thread can only compute one element at a time. To compute max over a row, we need all elements of that row. We could have each warp handle one row, and threads in the warp compute partial max/sum for a subset of c. Then warp shuffle reduce. But our threads are strided across (r,c). Alternatively, we can compute S and store it in a float array, then do a simple parallel loop to compute max and sum per row. Let's add a float smem `float s_s[Br][Bc+1]`. That's 64*65*4 = 16.6KB. Total smem = 107.9KB. Still okay for 2 blocks per SM. So smem layout: q_s: 64*129*2 = 16.5KB k_s: 64*129*2 = 16.5KB v_s: 64*129*2 = 16.5KB s_s: 64*65*4 = 16.6KB (float) p_s: 64*65*2 = 8.3KB (bf16) — actually do we need p_s if we write directly to global? We can write to global directly after computing P. So we don't need p_s in smem. o_acc: 64*129*4 = 33KB (float) m,l: 0.5KB Total = 99.1KB. Perfect. We compute S into s_s. Then reduce m and l using s_s. Then in pass 2, compute S again into s_s (or reuse? we could keep S_s from pass 1 if we don't overwrite, but we need V_s in pass 2, so we can recompute S. Recomputing S adds compute but saves reading K again. Actually we already read K in pass 1. In pass 2, we also need K to compute S. We can load K again or keep it in smem. Since we have s_s and v_s, we can load K_j again in pass 2. Memory bandwidth is plenty. But wait: if we load K_j in pass 2, we need to load it from global memory again. The total K reads would be 2x. That's fine. Actually, we can avoid recomputing S by saving the max and l and then in pass 2 we load K_j and compute S again. Or we can store S in global memory (that's P). But we want to compute P, so we need S anyway. Recomputing is easier. Revised pass 2: Loop over j_tile: Load K_j and V_j. Compute S into s_s. Compute P = exp(S - m) / l. Write P to global mem. Accumulate O += P @ V. This needs s_s (16.6KB), q_s, k_s, v_s, o_acc. Total ~91KB. Without p_s. Good. So we don't need p_s smem. Let's detail pass 1 and pass 2. **Pass 1:** For j_tile=0..N/Bc-1: Load K_j into k_s. __syncthreads(); Compute S into s_s: ``` for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float sum = 0.0f; for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += __bfloat162float(q_s[r * (D+1) + d]) * __bfloat162float(k_s[c * (D+1) + d]); } s_s[r * (Bc + 1) + c] = sum * scale; } __syncthreads(); // update m and l for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { float row_max = -INFINITY; for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { row_max = fmaxf(row_max, s_s[r * (Bc + 1) + c]); } float m_prev = m[r]; float m_new = fmaxf(m_prev, row_max); float alpha = expf(m_prev - m_new); float row_sum = 0.0f; for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { row_sum += expf(s_s[r * (Bc + 1) + c] - m_new); } l[r] = alpha * l[r] + row_sum; m[r] = m_new; } __syncthreads(); ``` **Pass 2:** For j_tile=0..N/Bc-1: Load K_j and V_j. __syncthreads(); // Compute S into s_s ... __syncthreads(); // Compute P, write global, accumulate O for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float p = expf(s_s[r*(Bc+1)+c] - m[r]) / l[r]; int p_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * N + j_start + c; p[p_idx] = __float2bfloat16(p); // We also need to accumulate O: but O is Br x D, so each (r,c) thread cannot accumulate all D dims. // We need a separate loop for O accumulation. But we have s_s and v_s. } __syncthreads(); // O accumulation: for each (r,d) for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; float sum = 0.0f; for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { float p = expf(s_s[r*(Bc+1)+c] - m[r]) / l[r]; sum += p * __bfloat162float(v_s[c*(D+1)+d]); } o_acc[r*(D+1)+d] += sum; } __syncthreads(); ``` Note: In the O accumulation loop, each thread recomputes P[r][c] for all c. That means each thread computes exp for each c. This is okay. But we can also store P in a float smem array to reuse. That would save recomputing exp. Since exp is relatively expensive, we might want to store P in `s_s` after overwriting S. But we need S for the next j_tile? No, each j_tile is independent. So we can compute S, then convert to P in place in s_s, then write to global, then use s_s for O accumulation. That avoids recomputing exp. Let's do: - After computing S in s_s, do: ``` for (idx): p = expf(s_s[idx] - m[r]) / l[r]; s_s[idx] = p; ``` - Then write p to global. - Then accumulate O using s_s as P. But we need v_s loaded. v_s is loaded before computing S? We can load V_j after computing S or before. Let's load V_j and K_j together at the start of pass 2 loop. Wait, we need K_j for S and V_j for O. So load both. ``` for j_tile: load K_j to k_s load V_j to v_s __syncthreads(); compute S to s_s __syncthreads(); // convert S to P in s_s for idx ...: s_s[idx] = expf(s_s[idx] - m[r]) / l[r]; p[p_idx] = __float2bfloat16(s_s[idx]); __syncthreads(); // accumulate O for idx ...: float sum = 0; for c: sum += s_s[r*(Bc+1)+c] * v_s[c*(D+1)+d]; o_acc[...] += sum; __syncthreads(); ``` This is efficient. After pass 2 loops, write O to global: ``` for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int o_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; o[o_idx] = __float2bfloat16(o_acc[r*(D+1)+d]); } ``` ### Kernel 2: `compute_d` Inputs: o, dO. Output: d (vector). Grid: (num_i_tiles, 1, B*Hq). Block: 256. ``` for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { float sum = 0.0f; for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += __bfloat162float(o[...]) * __bfloat162float(dout[...]); } d_vec[...] = sum; } ``` We can load o and dout into smem for faster access, but it's a small reduction. We can also read directly from global memory. To reduce global memory traffic, we can load a tile of o and dout (Br x D) into smem. Shared mem: 2 * 16.5KB = 33KB. Fine. ``` __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 o_s[Br*(D+1)]; __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 dO_s[Br*(D+1)]; // load for (idx) { o_s[idx] = o[...]; dO_s[idx] = dout[...]; } __syncthreads(); for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { ... use o_s, dO_s ... } ``` ### Kernel 3: `compute_ds` Inputs: dO, v, d_vec, p. Output: ds. Grid: (num_i_tiles, num_j_tiles, B*Hq). Block: 256. ``` int bh = blockIdx.z; int b = bh / Hq; int hq = bh % Hq; int kv = hq / (Hq / Hkv); int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int j_tile = blockIdx.y; int i_start = i_tile * Br; int j_start = j_tile * Bc; // load dO_i, V_j, D_i, P_ij // smem: dO_s[Br][D+1], V_s[Bc][D+1], P_s[Br][Bc+1], D_s[Br] ... __syncthreads(); for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float dp = 0.0f; for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { dp += __bfloat162float(dO_s[r*(D+1)+d]) * __bfloat162float(V_s[c*(D+1)+d]); } float p = __bfloat162float(P_s[r*(Bc+1)+c]); float ds = (dp - D_s[r]) * p; int ds_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * N + j_start + c; ds[ds_idx] = __float2bfloat16(ds); } ``` Wait, does dP = dO @ V^T use V from the KV head or the query head? In standard attention backward for GQA, each query head uses the shared KV head. So V_j belongs to `kv = hq / num_q_per_kv`. Yes. ### Kernel 4: `gemm_dQ` Inputs: ds, k. Output: dq. Grid: (num_i_tiles, 1, B*Hq). Block: 256. ``` int b = blockIdx.z / Hq; int hq = blockIdx.z % Hq; int kv = hq / (Hq / Hkv); int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int i_start = i_tile * Br; smem: ds_s[Br][Bc+1] float, K_s[Bc][D+1] bf16, dq_acc[Br][D+1] float. // zero dq_acc for ... dq_acc[idx] = 0; __syncthreads(); for (int j_tile = 0; j_tile < N / Bc; ++j_tile) { int j_start = j_tile * Bc; // load ds_ij for (idx) { ds_s[idx] = __bfloat162float(ds[...]); } // load K_j for (idx) { K_s[idx] = k[...]; } __syncthreads(); // accumulate dq_acc += ds_s @ K_s for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; float sum = 0.0f; for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { sum += ds_s[r*(Bc+1)+c] * __bfloat162float(K_s[c*(D+1)+d]); } dq_acc[r*(D+1)+d] += sum; } __syncthreads(); } // write dq for (idx) { dq[global] = __float2bfloat16(dq_acc[idx]); } ``` ### Kernel 5: `gemm_dV` Inputs: p, dO. Output: dv. Grid: (num_j_tiles, 1, B*Hkv). Block: 256. ``` int b = blockIdx.z / Hkv; int kv = blockIdx.z % Hkv; int j_tile = blockIdx.x; int j_start = j_tile * Bc; smem: P_s[Br][Bc+1] bf16, dO_s[Br][D+1] bf16, dv_acc[Bc][D+1] float. // zero dv_acc for ... dv_acc[idx] = 0; __syncthreads(); int num_q_per_kv = Hq / Hkv; int q_start = kv * num_q_per_kv; for (int qid = 0; qid < num_q_per_kv; ++qid) { int hq = q_start + qid; for (int i_tile = 0; i_tile < N / Br; ++i_tile) { int i_start = i_tile * Br; // load P_ij for idx: P_s[idx] = p[...]; // load dO_i for idx: dO_s[idx] = dout[...]; __syncthreads(); // accumulate dv_acc += P^T @ dO for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; float sum = 0.0f; for (int r = 0; r < Br; ++r) { sum += __bfloat162float(P_s[r*(Bc+1)+c]) * __bfloat162float(dO_s[r*(D+1)+d]); } dv_acc[c*(D+1)+d] += sum; } __syncthreads(); } } // write dv for idx: dv[global] = __float2bfloat16(dv_acc[idx]); ``` Wait, there is a subtlety: In the inner loop, `for (int r = 0; r < Br; ++r)` causes each thread to loop over r. If threads in a warp have different c, they access P_s[r*(Bc+1)+c] with different c. Since c varies across threads, and `r` is the same for all threads in the inner loop iteration, the access pattern is: all threads read the same r but different c. This corresponds to reading a row of P_s. P_s is stored row-major with padded columns. So `r*(Bc+1)+c` for fixed r and varying c: consecutive c correspond to consecutive elements. In bf16, consecutive elements are 2 bytes apart. Shared memory bank width is 4 bytes. So consecutive c values have 2-way bank conflicts (c=0,1 in bank0; c=2,3 in bank1, etc.). Since a warp reads 32 consecutive c values (if blockDim.x maps c as fastest), that's 16 banks, each accessed twice. So 2-way bank conflict. That's acceptable. But to improve, we could transpose P_s? Or store as float. If we store P_s as float with padding `float P_s[Br][Bc+1]`, row stride in bytes = (Bc+1)*4 = 260. 260/4 = 65. 65 mod 32 = 1. So consecutive c map to consecutive banks, no conflict. But storing as float uses 16KB. Fine. Let's store P_s as float to avoid conflicts. Similarly, dO_s can be float or bf16. If we store as float, we avoid conflicts in `dO_s[r*(D+1)+d]` when varying d? Wait, in dv accumulation, we have fixed c and varying r. We read `P_s[r*(Bc+1)+c]` (fixed c, varying r). Row stride = (Bc+1)*4 ≈ 256 bytes (for Bc=64). 65 words per row. 65 mod 32 = 1. So consecutive r map to bank diff 1. No conflict. Good. For `dO_s[r*(D+1)+d]` (fixed d, varying r): row stride = (D+1)*2? If bf16, row_stride_words = 129. 129 mod 32 = 1. So bank diff = 1. No conflict! Wait, earlier we computed for bf16 with row stride 258 bytes (129 elements * 2 bytes). (addr/2) % 32 = (base/2 + r*129 + d) % 32. 129 % 32 = 1. So bank diff is 1. No conflict for varying r and fixed d! The conflict was for varying c and fixed r in K_s and q_s. But in dv accumulation, we vary r, not c. So bf16 is fine here. Let's keep P_s as float because it's loaded from bf16 and used repeatedly. Actually, we can load bf16 and convert to float on the fly, storing as bf16 in smem. The issue is only when multiple threads in a warp read varying c in the same row. In dv accumulation, we vary r. So storing P_s as bf16 is fine and saves smem. Let's stick with bf16 for all inputs to save smem. Wait, in `compute_ds_kernel`, we compute dot product `dO_i[r] · V_j[c]`. Here we vary both r and c across threads. For dO_s, threads may have same r and varying d in the dot product loop (inner loop). But the dot product is sequential within a thread, not across threads. So no shared memory conflict from multiple threads. For V_s, threads have varying c and loop over d. In the dot product, each thread reads V_s[c][d] sequentially. Since c is fixed per thread, there is no conflict across threads. So bf16 is fine. In `gemm_dQ_kernel`, we have `ds_s[r][c] * K_s[c][d]`. ds_s is float, K_s is bf16. Threads vary r and d. For a given `idx`, r and d are fixed. The loop is over c. Each thread reads K_s[c][d] for varying c and fixed d. Since d is fixed per thread, and c varies, the address is `c*(D+1)+d`. This is the same pattern we analyzed: varying c, fixed d for K_s. With row stride (D+1)*2 ≈ 258 bytes. Bank = (base/2 + c*129 + d) % 32. Since 129%32=1, consecutive c map to consecutive banks. No conflict! Wait, we previously thought varying c with fixed d hits same bank? Let's recalc carefully. Shared memory bank index for address `A` (in bytes): For compute capability >= 7.0, bank width is 4 bytes. Bank index = (A / 4) % 32 if access is 32 bits or larger. For 16-bit accesses, it's more complex. Actually, for 16-bit access, the address is divided by 2 to get a 16-bit word index? Let's check NVIDIA docs. For compute capability 9.0: - Shared memory has 32 banks. - Successive 32-bit words are assigned to successive banks. - For 64-bit accesses, bank = (addr / 8) % 32? Actually each 64-bit access uses two banks. - For 16-bit accesses: The bank index is (addr / 2) % 32? No. Let's recall: On Maxwell/Pascal, bank width is 4 bytes. A 32-bit word occupies one bank. Two 16-bit accesses to addresses that differ by 2 bytes are in the same bank. The bank index for address `addr` is `(addr / 4) % 32` for 32-bit access. For 16-bit access, the bank index is effectively `(addr / 2) % 32`? Actually, if bank width is 4 bytes, then addresses 0-1 are bank0, 2-3 are bank0? No, each bank holds 4 bytes. So address `A` maps to bank `(A / 4) % 32`. Two 16-bit values at addresses 0 and 2 are both in bank 0. So consecutive 16-bit accesses (stride 2) hit the same bank. That's the 2-way conflict. However, if we access with stride `(D+1)*2` where D+1=129, then the address increment is 258 bytes. Bank change = (258/4) % 32 = 64 % 32 = 0. So row stride of 258 bytes means each row starts at the same bank! But we add `d` offset. For fixed d, varying c: address = base + c*258 + d. Bank = ((base + d)/4 + c*64) % 32 = ((base+d)/4) % 32. So all rows map to the same bank for the same d! This is because 258 is a multiple of 4? 258/2 = 129, which is odd. But 258 bytes is not a multiple of 4? 258 = 4*64 + 2. So 258 is not a multiple of 4. Wait: row size in bytes = (D+1)*2 = 258. 258 % 4 = 2. So the bank for row c is: bank(c) = floor((base + c*258 + d) / 4) % 32. c*258 = c*(256+2) = c*256 + c*2. c*256 is divisible by 4 (since 256%4=0). So bank(c) = floor((base + c*2 + d)/4) % 32. Since c*2 increments by 2 per row, floor(.../4) increments by 0 or 1. Specifically, for even c, c*2 is multiple of 4, so bank increments by 0. For odd c, c*2 = 2 mod 4, so floor increments by 0 if (base+d)%4 < 2, else by 1? Let's compute: Let B = base + d. Then address = B + 2c. floor((B + 2c)/4) = floor(B/4 + c/2). For c even: c=2k => floor(B/4 + k) = floor(B/4) + k. For c odd: c=2k+1 => floor(B/4 + k + 0.5) = floor(B/4) + k + floor(frac(B/4) + 0.5). So bank increments by 1 every two rows, but sometimes it's the same for two rows. Overall, row stride causes bank to increase by 1 for every 2 rows. So across 32 threads with c=0..31, the banks are not all distinct. There will be some conflicts, but not 32-way. It might be 2-way conflict. Let's test: Suppose B%4 = 0. c=0: floor(0/4)=0 c=1: floor(2/4)=0 c=2: floor(4/4)=1 c=3: floor(6/4)=1 c=4: floor(8/4)=2 ... So banks are 0,0,1,1,2,2,3,3,... So each bank is shared by two rows. For a warp accessing same d but c=0..31, there are 16 distinct banks, each accessed twice. So 2-way bank conflict. That's manageable. If B%4 = 2: c=0: floor(2/4)=0 c=1: floor(4/4)=1 c=2: floor(6/4)=1 c=3: floor(8/4)=2 c=4: floor(10/4)=2 ... Banks: 0,1,1,2,2,3,... Same pattern: 2-way conflict. So padding with 1 element for bf16 leads to 2-way bank conflict when accessing same d across different c. That's acceptable. If we pad with 2 elements (D+2), row size = 130*2 = 260 bytes. 260%4=0. Then bank increments by (260/4)%32 = 65%32 = 1. So no conflict at all! Great. Let's use pitch = D+2 for bf16 arrays. That is, each row has D+2 elements. Then there's no bank conflict. Let's adopt that. For float arrays, pitch = D+1 is enough because 4 bytes per element. Row size = (D+1)*4 = 516 bytes. 516%4=0. Bank increment = (516/4)%32 = 129%32 = 1. No conflict. Good. So for bf16 arrays: pitch = D+2. For float arrays: pitch = D+1. Let's recalculate smem sizes with these pitches. `compute_p_and_o`: q_s: Br * (D+2) * 2 bytes. k_s: Bc * (D+2) * 2. v_s: Bc * (D+2) * 2. s_s: Br * (Bc+1) * 4. o_acc: Br * (D+1) * 4. m,l: Br * 4 * 2. With Br=64, Bc=64, D=128: q_s: 64*130*2 = 16,640 bytes k_s: 64*130*2 = 16,640 v_s: 64*130*2 = 16,640 s_s: 64*65*4 = 16,640 o_acc: 64*129*4 = 33,024 m,l: 512 Total = 100,096 bytes ≈ 97.75 KB. 2 blocks per SM = 195.5KB < 228KB. Good. `compute_ds_kernel`: dO_s: 64*130*2 = 16,640 V_s: 64*130*2 = 16,640 P_s: 64*66*2 = 8,448 (since Bc+2 = 66) D_s: 64*4 = 256 Total = 41,984 bytes ≈ 41KB. 5 blocks per SM = 205KB < 228KB. Great. Wait, for P_s in `compute_ds_kernel`, we need to load P_ij from global memory. We can store it as bf16 with pitch Bc+2: `P_s[r*(Bc+2)+c]`. That's fine. Row size = 66*2 = 132 bytes. 132%4=0? 132/4=33. 33%32=1. So no conflict for varying r or c. Actually varying c: stride=2 bytes, so 2-way conflict if we access consecutive c across threads. But we'll access P_s with varying r and c. In `compute_ds_kernel`, each thread computes one (r,c). No conflict across threads because each thread handles a unique pair. But when reading for O accumulation later, we do sequential reads. So it's fine. `gemm_dQ_kernel`: ds_s: float[Br][Bc+1] = 64*65*4 = 16,640 K_s: bf16[Bc][D+2] = 64*130*2 = 16,640 dq_acc: float[Br][D+1] = 33,024 Total = 66,304 bytes ≈ 64.75 KB. 3 blocks per SM = 194KB. Good. `gemm_dV_kernel`: P_s: bf16[Br][Bc+2] = 64*66*2 = 8,448 dO_s: bf16[Br][D+2] = 16,640 dv_acc: float[Bc][D+1] = 33,024 Total = 58,112 bytes ≈ 56.75 KB. 4 blocks per SM = 227KB? 4*58,112 = 232,448 > 228KB. So 3 blocks per SM = 174KB. Fine. `gemm_dK_kernel`: dS_s: float[Br][Bc+1] = 16,640 Q_s: bf16[Br][D+2] = 16,640 dk_acc: float[Bc][D+1] = 33,024 Total = 66,304 bytes. Same as dQ. 3 blocks per SM. So occupancy is reasonable. Now let's think about whether we need to write separate kernels for dK and dV. We could combine them into one kernel to reduce overhead. They both loop over qid and i_tile. But combining might increase register pressure. Given we have 64 blocks for each, maybe it's fine to keep separate. But 256 blocks per kernel may have low occupancy. Wait, dV blocks = B*Hkv*(N/Bc) = 2*8*16 = 256. With 3 blocks per SM, we can run 256/3 ≈ 85 SMs, which is less than 132. So some SMs are idle. But the kernel iterates over 64 inner tiles, so each block does a lot of work. The time might be dominated by the inner loops, so idle SMs might not matter much because the active SMs are fully utilized. But to better utilize the GPU, we can reduce Bc to 32 to get more blocks: 2*8*32 = 512. Then with 3 blocks per SM, we need 171 SMs. Good. But Bc=32 means K_s is smaller (32*130*2 = 8,320). dv_acc is 32*129*4 = 16,512. P_s is 64*34*2 = 4,352. dO_s is 64*130*2 = 16,640. Total = 45,824 bytes. 5 blocks per SM = 229KB > 228KB. So 4 blocks per SM = 183KB. 512 blocks / 4 = 128 SMs. Good. But inner loop over c is 32, which reduces work per iteration. It's a tradeoff. Let's keep Bc=64 for simplicity. 256 blocks is okay. H100 is fast. Actually, we can also split the D dimension for dV and dK to get more blocks. For example, each block computes a Bc x 64 tile of D. With D=128, we have 2 tiles. Then blocks = 256 * 2 = 512. That would fill the GPU better. Let's do that: For `gemm_dV_kernel` and `gemm_dK_kernel`, grid = (num_j_tiles, D_TILES, B*Hkv). Where D_TILES = D / D_TILE_SIZE = 128 / 64 = 2. Then dv_acc is Bc x 64 float = 64*65*4 = 16,640. dO_s is Br x 130 x 2 = 16,640. P_s is Br x 66 x 2 = 8,448. Total = 41,728 bytes. 5 blocks per SM = 208KB. 512 blocks / 5 = 103 SMs. Good. Each block accumulates into a sub-tile of D. But writing to global memory requires writing only the sub-tile. That adds some complexity but manageable. However, given the complexity of writing all these kernels, and that we need to output code that compiles, maybe we should avoid splitting D for dK/dV. Let's keep Bc=64 and D full. 256 blocks might be okay. The inner loop does 64*16*4 = 1024 iterations of loading and accumulating. Each block does ~64M FLOPs. Total 16B FLOPs. At 100 TFLOPS, 0.16ms. So even with some idle SMs, it's fine. Let's proceed with Bc=64 and Br=64. One more thing: `compute_p_and_o` pass 1 needs to compute S. But we have q_s and k_s. The dot product length is D=128. We can `#pragma unroll` the loop. 128 is small. Unrolling might help. Also, we need to be careful with `expf`. We can use `expf` from libdevice. That's fine. Let's also think about `blockIdx.z` mapping for grid dimensions. The maximum grid dimension on Hopper is large. 3D grid up to (2^31-1, 65535, 65535). So grid.z=64 is fine. For `compute_ds_kernel`, grid = (16, 16, 64). That's fine. Now, write the C++ code for the kernels. We need to handle `BATCH`, `NUM_HEADS`, `NUM_KV_HEADS`, `SEQ_LEN`, `HEAD_DIM` as constants or variables. The kernel launch will use the variables passed from Python. Since these are known at runtime but fixed for the problem, we can pass them as args or use templates. Templates are better for performance because the compiler can unroll loops, but the sizes are dynamic (B, Hq, Hkv, N, D). However, we know N=1024, D=128, B=2, Hq=32, Hkv=8 from the problem, but we shouldn't hardcode them because the kernel code should be generic. But we can hardcode tile sizes Br=64, Bc=64, D=128 as template parameters for better optimization. Actually, to keep code simple and generic, we can pass N, D, etc. as integer args and use runtime loops. Since D=128 is a runtime variable but known, the compiler might not unroll the inner loop fully. But we can add `#pragma unroll 8` or similar. Alternatively, we can template on HEAD_DIM=128 and Br=64, Bc=64. Since the problem says HEAD_DIM=128 is fixed, templating is fine. Let's template the kernels on `Br`, `Bc`, `D` to allow compiler optimization. `template <int Br, int Bc, int D>`. Wait, we also have `N` which might not be a multiple of Br. But N=1024, Br=64, so it's exact. We can assume multiples for simplicity, but add checks if we want. Let's write the kernel code. Also note: `__nv_bfloat16` arithmetic operations are limited. We use `__bfloat162float` and `__float2bfloat16`. In global memory loads/stores, we just load/store `__nv_bfloat16`. Let's draft the full C++ code. ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cuda_bf16.h> #include <math.h> #define PITCH_BF16(D) (D + 2) #define PITCH_F(D) (D + 1) template <int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void compute_p_and_o_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* q, const __nv_bfloat16* k, const __nv_bfloat16* v, __nv_bfloat16* p, __nv_bfloat16* o, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, float scale) { const int q_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int k_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int v_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int s_pitch = Bc + 1; const int o_pitch = PITCH_F(D); int b = blockIdx.z / Hq; int hq = blockIdx.z % Hq; int kv = hq / (Hq / Hkv); int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int i_start = i_tile * Br; int num_j_tiles = N / Bc; extern __shared__ char smem[]; __nv_bfloat16* q_s = (__nv_bfloat16*)smem; __nv_bfloat16* k_s = q_s + Br * q_pitch; __nv_bfloat16* v_s = k_s + Bc * k_pitch; float* s_s = (float*)(v_s + Bc * v_pitch); float* o_acc = s_s + Br * s_pitch; float* m = o_acc + Br * o_pitch; float* l = m + Br; // Load Q_i for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int q_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; q_s[r * q_pitch + d] = q[q_idx]; } if (threadIdx.x < Br) { int r = threadIdx.x; m[r] = -INFINITY; l[r] = 0.0f; } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { o_acc[idx] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); // Pass 1: compute row max and sum for softmax for (int j_tile = 0; j_tile < num_j_tiles; ++j_tile) { int j_start = j_tile * Bc; // Load K_j for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int k_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; k_s[c * k_pitch + d] = k[k_idx]; } __syncthreads(); // Compute S = Q_i * K_j^T * scale for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += __bfloat162float(q_s[r * q_pitch + d]) * __bfloat162float(k_s[c * k_pitch + d]); } s_s[r * s_pitch + c] = sum * scale; } __syncthreads(); // Update running max and sum for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { float row_max = -INFINITY; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { row_max = fmaxf(row_max, s_s[r * s_pitch + c]); } float m_prev = m[r]; float m_new = fmaxf(m_prev, row_max); float alpha = expf(m_prev - m_new); float row_sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { row_sum += expf(s_s[r * s_pitch + c] - m_new); } l[r] = alpha * l[r] + row_sum; m[r] = m_new; } __syncthreads(); } // Pass 2: compute P and O for (int j_tile = 0; j_tile < num_j_tiles; ++j_tile) { int j_start = j_tile * Bc; // Load K_j and V_j for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int k_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; k_s[c * k_pitch + d] = k[k_idx]; int v_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; v_s[c * v_pitch + d] = v[v_idx]; } __syncthreads(); // Compute S for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += __bfloat162float(q_s[r * q_pitch + d]) * __bfloat162float(k_s[c * k_pitch + d]); } s_s[r * s_pitch + c] = sum * scale; } __syncthreads(); // Convert S to P and write to global memory for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float p_val = expf(s_s[r * s_pitch + c] - m[r]) / l[r]; s_s[r * s_pitch + c] = p_val; // reuse as P in smem int p_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * N + j_start + c; p[p_idx] = __float2bfloat16(p_val); } __syncthreads(); // Accumulate O += P @ V_j for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; float sum = o_acc[r * o_pitch + d]; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { sum += s_s[r * s_pitch + c] * __bfloat162float(v_s[c * v_pitch + d]); } o_acc[r * o_pitch + d] = sum; } __syncthreads(); } // Write O to global memory for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int o_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; o[o_idx] = __float2bfloat16(o_acc[r * o_pitch + d]); } } ``` `compute_d_kernel`: ```cpp template <int Br, int D> __global__ void compute_d_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* o, const __nv_bfloat16* dout, float* d_vec, int B, int Hq, int N) { const int o_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); int b = blockIdx.z / Hq; int hq = blockIdx.z % Hq; int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int i_start = i_tile * Br; extern __shared__ char smem[]; __nv_bfloat16* o_s = (__nv_bfloat16*)smem; __nv_bfloat16* dO_s = o_s + Br * o_pitch; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int o_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; o_s[r * o_pitch + d] = o[o_idx]; dO_s[r * o_pitch + d] = dout[o_idx]; } __syncthreads(); for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { float sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += __bfloat162float(o_s[r * o_pitch + d]) * __bfloat162float(dO_s[r * o_pitch + d]); } d_vec[(b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r] = sum; } } ``` `compute_ds_kernel`: ```cpp template <int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void compute_ds_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* dout, const __nv_bfloat16* v, const float* d_vec, const __nv_bfloat16* p, __nv_bfloat16* ds, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N) { const int dO_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int v_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int p_pitch = PITCH_BF16(Bc); // Actually P is stored as [N][Bc] in the tile? Wait P global is [N][N] so pitch is N. // But in smem we use padded layout. const int smem_p_pitch = Bc + 2; int bh = blockIdx.z; int b = bh / Hq; int hq = bh % Hq; int kv = hq / (Hq / Hkv); int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int j_tile = blockIdx.y; int i_start = i_tile * Br; int j_start = j_tile * Bc; extern __shared__ char smem[]; __nv_bfloat16* dO_s = (__nv_bfloat16*)smem; __nv_bfloat16* v_s = dO_s + Br * dO_pitch; __nv_bfloat16* p_s = v_s + Bc * v_pitch; float* d_s = (float*)(p_s + Br * smem_p_pitch); // Load dO_i for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int dO_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; dO_s[r * dO_pitch + d] = dout[dO_idx]; } // Load V_j for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int v_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; v_s[c * v_pitch + d] = v[v_idx]; } // Load D_i for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { d_s[r] = d_vec[(b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r]; } // Load P_ij for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; int p_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * N + j_start + c; p_s[r * smem_p_pitch + c] = p[p_idx]; } __syncthreads(); for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float dp = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { dp += __bfloat162float(dO_s[r * dO_pitch + d]) * __bfloat162float(v_s[c * v_pitch + d]); } } ``` `gemm_dq_kernel`: ```cpp template <int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void gemm_dq_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* ds, const __nv_bfloat16* k, __nv_bfloat16* dq, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N) { const int ds_pitch = Bc + 1; // float const int k_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int dq_pitch = PITCH_F(D); int b = blockIdx.z / Hq; int hq = blockIdx.z % Hq; int kv = hq / (Hq / Hkv); int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int i_start = i_tile * Br; int num_j_tiles = N / Bc; extern __shared__ char smem[]; float* ds_s = (float*)smem; __nv_bfloat16* k_s = (__nv_bfloat16*)(ds_s + Br * ds_pitch); float* dq_acc = (float*)(k_s + Bc * k_pitch); // zero dq_acc for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { dq_acc[idx] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); for (int j_tile = 0; j_tile < num_j_tiles; ++j_tile) { int j_start = j_tile * Bc; // Load ds_ij for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; int ds_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * N + j_start + c; ds_s[r * ds_pitch + c] = __bfloat162float(ds[ds_idx]); } // Load K_j for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int k_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; k_s[c * k_pitch + d] = k[k_idx]; } __syncthreads(); // Accumulate for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; float sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { sum += ds_s[r * ds_pitch + c] * __bfloat162float(k_s[c * k_pitch + d]); } dq_acc[r * dq_pitch + d] += sum; } __syncthreads(); } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int dq_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; dq[dq_idx] = __float2bfloat16(dq_acc[r * dq_pitch + d]); } } ``` `gemm_dv_kernel`: ```cpp template <int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void gemm_dv_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* p, const __nv_bfloat16* dout, __nv_bfloat16* dv, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N) { const int p_pitch = PITCH_BF16(Bc); // smem pitch const int dO_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int dv_pitch = PITCH_F(D); int b = blockIdx.z / Hkv; int kv = blockIdx.z % Hkv; int j_tile = blockIdx.x; int j_start = j_tile * Bc; int num_q_per_kv = Hq / Hkv; // zero dv_acc for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { dv_acc[idx] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); for (int qid = 0; qid < num_q_per_kv; ++qid) { int hq = q_start + qid; for (int i_tile = 0; p_s[r * p_pitch + c] = p[p_idx]; } // Load dO_i for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int dO_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; dO_s[r * dO_pitch + d] = dout[dO_idx]; } __syncthreads(); // Accumulate dv_acc += P^T @ dO for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; float sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int r = 0; r < Br; ++r) { sum += __bfloat162float(p_s[r * p_pitch + c]) * __bfloat162float(dO_s[r * dO_pitch + d]); } dv_acc[c * dv_pitch + d] += sum; } __syncthreads(); } } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int dv_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; dv[dv_idx] = __float2bfloat16(dv_acc[c * dv_pitch + d]); } } ``` `gemm_dk_kernel`: ```cpp template <int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void gemm_dk_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* ds, const __nv_bfloat16* q, __nv_bfloat16* dk, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N) { const int ds_pitch = Bc + 1; // float const int q_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int dk_pitch = PITCH_F(D); int b = blockIdx.z / Hkv; int kv = blockIdx.z % Hkv; int j_tile = blockIdx.x; int j_start = j_tile * Bc; int num_q_per_kv = Hq / Hkv; int q_start = kv * num_q_per_kv; int num_i_tiles = N / Br; extern __shared__ char smem[]; float* ds_s = (float*)smem; __nv_bfloat16* q_s = (__nv_bfloat16*)(ds_s + Br * ds_pitch); float* dk_acc = (float*)(q_s + Br * q_pitch); // zero dk_acc for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { dk_acc[idx] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); for (int qid = 0; qid < num_q_per_kv; ++qid) { int hq = q_start + qid; for (int i_tile = 0; i_tile < num_i_tiles; ++i_tile) { int i_start = i_tile * Br; // Load ds_ij for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; int ds_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * N + j_start + c; ds_s[r * ds_pitch + c] = __bfloat162float(ds[ds_idx]); } // Load Q_i for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int q_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; q_s[r * q_pitch + d] = q[q_idx]; } __syncthreads(); // Accumulate dk_acc += ds^T @ Q for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; float sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int r = 0; r < Br; ++r) { sum += ds_s[r * ds_pitch + c] * __bfloat162float(q_s[r * q_pitch + d]); } dk_acc[c * dk_pitch + d] += sum; } __syncthreads(); } } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int dk_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; dk[dk_idx] = __float2bfloat16(dk_acc[c * dk_pitch + d]); } } ``` Now we need to define the C++ wrapper functions that launch these kernels and are bound to Python. ```cpp std::vector<torch::Tensor> attention_backward( torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v, torch::Tensor dout) { // Check inputs int B = q.size(0); int Hq = q.size(1); int N = q.size(2); int D = q.size(3); int Hkv = k.size(1); auto opts = q.options(); torch::Tensor p = torch::empty({B, Hq, N, N}, opts); torch::Tensor o = torch::empty({B, Hq, N, D}, opts); torch::Tensor d_vec = torch::empty({B, Hq, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kFloat32).device(q.device())); torch::Tensor ds = torch::empty({B, Hq, N, N}, opts); torch::Tensor dq = torch::empty_like(q); torch::Tensor dk = torch::empty_like(k); torch::Tensor dv = torch::empty_like(v); const int Br = 64; const int Bc = 64; float scale = 1.0f / sqrtf((float)D); // Kernel 1: compute P and O dim3 grid1(N / Br, 1, B * Hq); int smem1 = (Br * (D + 2) + Bc * (D + 2) + Bc * (D + 2)) * sizeof(__nv_bfloat16) + Br * (Bc + 1) * sizeof(float) + Br * (D + 1) * sizeof(float) + Br * 2 * sizeof(float); compute_p_and_o_kernel<Br, Bc, D><<<grid1, 256, smem1>>>( (__nv_bfloat16*)q.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)k.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)v.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)p.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)o.data_ptr(), B, Hq, Hkv, N, scale); // Kernel 2: compute D dim3 grid2(N / Br, 1, B * Hq); int smem2 = 2 * Br * (D + 2) * sizeof(__nv_bfloat16); compute_d_kernel<Br, D><<<grid2, 256, smem2>>>( (__nv_bfloat16*)o.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)dout.data_ptr(), (float*)d_vec.data_ptr(), B, Hq, N); // Kernel 3: compute dS dim3 grid3(N / Br, N / Bc, B * Hq); int smem3 = (Br * (D + 2) + Bc * (D + 2) + Br * (Bc + 2)) * sizeof(__nv_bfloat16) + Br * sizeof(float); compute_ds_kernel<Br, Bc, D><<<grid3, 256, smem3>>>( (__nv_bfloat16*)dout.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)v.data_ptr(), (float*)d_vec.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)p.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)ds.data_ptr(), B, Hq, Hkv, N); // Kernel 4: compute dQ dim3 grid4(N / Br, 1, B * Hq); int smem4 = Br * (Bc + 1) * sizeof(float) + Bc * (D + 2) * sizeof(__nv_bfloat16) + Br * (D + 1) * sizeof(float); gemm_dq_kernel<Br, Bc, D><<<grid4, 256, smem4>>>( (__nv_bfloat16*)ds.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)k.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)dq.data_ptr(), B, Hq, Hkv, N); // Kernel 5: compute dK dim3 grid5(N / Bc, 1, B * Hkv); int smem5 = Br * (Bc + 1) * sizeof(float) + Br * (D + 2) * sizeof(__nv_bfloat16) + Bc * (D + 1) * sizeof(float); gemm_dk_kernel<Br, Bc, D><<<grid5, 256, smem5>>>( (__nv_bfloat16*)ds.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)q.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)dk.data_ptr(), B, Hq, Hkv, N); // Kernel 6: compute dV dim3 grid6(N / Bc, 1, B * Hkv); int smem6 = Br * (Bc + 2) * sizeof(__nv_bfloat16) + Br * (D + 2) * sizeof(__nv_bfloat16) + Bc * (D + 1) * sizeof(float); gemm_dv_kernel<Br, Bc, D><<<grid6, 256, smem6>>>( (__nv_bfloat16*)p.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)dout.data_ptr(), (__nv_bfloat16*)dv.data_ptr(), B, Hq, Hkv, N); return {dq, dk, dv}; } ``` Wait, the `torch::Tensor` uses `at::BFloat16` in C++ land if the tensor is bf16. But we cast pointers to `__nv_bfloat16*`. `at::BFloat16` and `__nv_bfloat16` are layout-compatible (both 16-bit). So this is okay. We need to make sure we use `cudaDeviceSynchronize()` or rely on the default stream? PyTorch CUDA ops run on the current stream. So kernel launches are async. We don't need to sync. But for correctness, the kernels are in order. We also need to handle `TORCH_CUDA_KERNEL_LAUNCH_CHECK`? Not strictly necessary but good for debug. Now, `load_inline` code. One subtlety: In the forward softmax, we do: ``` alpha = exp(m_prev - m_new); l[r] = alpha * l[r] + row_sum; ``` This is the standard online softmax. But is it numerically stable? Yes. No conflict. `k_s` is accessed with varying c and fixed d. `k_pitch = D+2`. row_stride_bytes = (D+2)*2 = 260 bytes. For bf16, bank width is 4 bytes. As analyzed earlier, with row_stride=260 bytes (65 words of 4 bytes), bank increments by 1 mod 32 for every 2 rows? Wait, for bf16, each element is 2 bytes. Address for k_s[c*k_pitch + d] = base + c*260 + d*2. Bank = floor((base + c*260 + d*2) / 4) % 32. 260/4 = 65. So floor(.../4) = floor(base/4) + c*65 + floor((d*2 + (base%4)) / 4). Since d is fixed per thread, the term `floor((d*2 + base%4)/4)` is constant. As c increases by 1, floor(.../4) increases by 65. 65 % 32 = 1. So consecutive c map to banks differing by 1. Thus varying c across the loop (sequential within a thread) has no bank conflict. But what about across threads? In this loop, each thread has its own c loop. Threads are not accessing the same c simultaneously. Actually, in a given clock cycle, all threads in a warp are at the same c iteration (since c is uniform across the loop? No, c is the loop variable, same for all threads in the warp because there's no thread-dependent conditional on c. So all threads in a warp read `k_s[c * k_pitch + d]` for the same c but different d. Different d means different offsets within the row. Since the row is contiguous in memory (bf16), addresses differ by d*2. For 32 threads with consecutive d, addresses differ by 2 bytes. Bank width is 4 bytes, so pairs of threads share banks. But does `k_s[c * k_pitch + d]` get broadcast? No, each thread reads a different address. Since consecutive d differ by 2 bytes, threads with d=0,1 are in the same bank (bank conflict 2-way), d=2,3 in next bank, etc. So there is a 2-way bank conflict for k_s loads when threads access consecutive d. Is that a big issue? Probably fine. We can avoid it by storing k_s in float (4 bytes) so each thread maps to a distinct bank. But that doubles smem for k_s from 16KB to 32KB. Still fine. Let's do that for the GEMM kernels to be safe. Actually, for `gemm_dq_kernel`, k_s is read in the inner loop (varying c, fixed d). If d varies across threads in a warp, there is 2-way conflict for bf16. If we store k_s as float, conflict is gone. Let's use float for k_s in `gemm_dq_kernel`. Wait, we need to load k from global (bf16) and convert. So float k_s[Bc][D+1] uses `Bc*(D+1)*4` bytes. For Bc=64, D=128: 64*129*4 = 33KB. That's double but still okay. Total smem4 = ds_s 16.6KB + k_s 33KB + dq_acc 33KB = 82.6KB. 2 blocks per SM. Fine. Similarly, for `gemm_dk_kernel`, q_s is read with varying r, fixed d. If q_s is bf16, threads access same d but varying r. For `q_s[r * q_pitch + d]` with fixed d, varying r: row_stride = (D+2)*2 = 260 bytes. As analyzed, bank changes by 1 every 2 rows? Actually floor((base + r*260 + d*2)/4) = floor(base/4) + r*65 + floor((d*2 + base%4)/4). Since 65%32=1, banks differ by 1 per row. So no conflict. So q_s can be bf16. For `gemm_dv_kernel`, dO_s is read with varying r, fixed d. Same as q_s, bf16 is fine. But for `gemm_dq_kernel`, k_s is problematic. Let's convert k_s to float. Actually, wait: in `gemm_dq_kernel`, the inner loop is: ``` for c: sum += ds_s[r * ds_pitch + c] * float(k_s[c * k_pitch + d]); ``` All threads in a warp have the same c at each step. ds_s is accessed with fixed r and varying c. Since ds_s is float and row_stride = 65*4=260, banks differ by 1 per c. No conflict. k_s is accessed with varying c and fixed d. Since d is fixed per thread, and c is uniform across the warp, at each step all threads read the same row c but different columns d. In smem, this is a row access with consecutive columns. For bf16, consecutive columns have 2-way conflict. For float, no conflict. So let's allocate `float k_s[Bc][D+1]` in `gemm_dq_kernel`. Same for `gemm_dk_kernel`? In `gemm_dk_kernel`, the inner loop is: ``` for r: sum += ds_s[r * ds_pitch + c] * float(q_s[r * q_pitch + d]); ``` Here, ds_s is accessed with varying r, fixed c. Since ds_s row_stride=260 (float), banks differ by 1 per r. No conflict. q_s is accessed with varying r, fixed d. As analyzed, bf16 is fine. So `gemm_dk_kernel` can keep q_s as bf16. Similarly for `gemm_dv_kernel`: ``` for r: sum += float(p_s[r * p_pitch + c]) * float(dO_s[r * dO_pitch + d]); ``` p_s is bf16 accessed with fixed c, varying r. Row_stride = (Bc+2)*2 = 132 bytes. 132/4 = 33. 33%32=1. So banks differ by 1 per r. No conflict. dO_s is bf16 accessed with varying r, fixed d. Fine. So only `gemm_dq_kernel` needs k_s in float to avoid conflict. But we could also keep k_s as bf16 and accept a 2-way conflict. 2-way conflict is not terrible. But we have smem. Let's just use float for k_s to be safe. Alternatively, we can transpose k_s when loading! But that's more complex. Let's keep k_s as float for `gemm_dq_kernel`. We'll adjust smem size. Wait, `gemm_dq_kernel` currently casts k to bf16 when loading. If k_s is float, we load `k_s[c * k_pitch + d] = static_cast<float>(k[...])`. And in the inner loop, we just do `k_s[c * k_pitch + d]`. `k_pitch` is D+1. Good. Now, what about `gemm_dk_kernel`? It has ds_s (float) and q_s (bf16). It reads ds_s with fixed c, varying r. ds_s is float with row_stride = (Bc+1)*4 = 260. Banks differ by 1 per r. Good. q_s is bf16 with varying r, fixed d. Fine. So only `gemm_dq_kernel` change. Let's also consider using `__ldg` for global memory loads? Might help but not necessary. Now let's write the code. Actually, there is one more thing: `p_s` pitch in `gemm_dv_kernel`. We used `p_pitch = PITCH_BF16(Bc)` = Bc+2. That's 66. Row size = 132 bytes. 132/4 = 33. 33%32 = 1. So banks differ by 1 per r. Good. `dO_s` pitch = D+2 = 130. Row size = 260 bytes. 260/4 = 65. 65%32 = 1. Banks differ by 1 per r. Good. `q_s` pitch in `gemm_dk_kernel` = D+2 = 130. Same. Good. `v_s` pitch in `compute_ds_kernel` = D+2 = 130. Good. `p_s` pitch in `compute_ds_kernel` = Bc+2 = 66. Good. `o_s` and `dO_s` in `compute_d_kernel` pitch = D+2. Good. So bank conflicts are minimized. Now, let's implement the kernels. I will write the code carefully. Wait, one issue: `at::BFloat16` conversion to float may not be as simple as `float(x)` in all contexts? Actually `at::BFloat16` has `operator float() const`. So `static_cast<float>(x)` or `float(x)` works. Let's write the code using `at::BFloat16`. I should also add `#include <cuda_runtime.h>` and `#include <math.h>`. Let's also be careful with `blockIdx.z` and grid dimensions. In CUDA, grid dimensions are `dim3`. If we pass `B * Hq` as an integer, it will be implicitly converted to `dim3` with x=val, y=1, z=1. But we want z to be the batch dimension. Actually, in our launch we wrote `dim3 grid1(N / Br, 1, B * Hq)`. That's a 3D grid. `blockIdx.z` ranges from 0 to B*Hq-1. Good. `dim3 grid3(N / Br, N / Bc, B * Hq);` blockIdx.x is i_tile, blockIdx.y is j_tile, blockIdx.z is bh. Good. Now, `compute_d_kernel` uses `blockIdx.x` for i_tile, `blockIdx.z` for b,hq. Let's write the code. One more thing: we need to ensure that PyTorch tensors are contiguous. The inputs might be non-contiguous? `torch.randn(...)` creates contiguous tensors. But to be safe, we can check `.is_contiguous()` or call `.contiguous()` in the Python wrapper. The reference likely provides contiguous tensors. We can call `.contiguous()` on inputs in `forward` to be safe. Also, `q, k, v, dout` have `requires_grad=True`? In `get_inputs`, q,k,v have `requires_grad=True`, dout does not. In our `forward`, we compute grads. We don't need to set `requires_grad` on outputs. We'll just return the gradient tensors. Let's draft the final Python code. ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline CUDA_SOURCE = r""" #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <math.h> #define PITCH_BF16(D) ((D) + 2) #define PITCH_F(D) ((D) + 1) template <int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void compute_p_and_o_kernel( const at::BFloat16* q, const at::BFloat16* k, const at::BFloat16* v, at::BFloat16* __restrict__ p, at::BFloat16* __restrict__ o, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, float scale) { const int q_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int k_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int v_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int s_pitch = Bc + 1; const int o_pitch = PITCH_F(D); int b = blockIdx.z / Hq; int hq = blockIdx.z % Hq; int kv = hq / (Hq / Hkv); int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int i_start = i_tile * Br; int num_j_tiles = N / Bc; extern __shared__ char smem[]; at::BFloat16* q_s = (at::BFloat16*)smem; at::BFloat16* k_s = q_s + Br * q_pitch; at::BFloat16* v_s = k_s + Bc * k_pitch; float* s_s = (float*)(v_s + Bc * v_pitch); float* o_acc = s_s + Br * s_pitch; float* m = o_acc + Br * o_pitch; float* l = m + Br; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int q_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; q_s[r * q_pitch + d] = q[q_idx]; } if (threadIdx.x < Br) { int r = threadIdx.x; m[r] = -INFINITY; l[r] = 0.0f; } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { o_acc[idx] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); // Pass 1 for (int j_tile = 0; j_tile < num_j_tiles; ++j_tile) { int j_start = j_tile * Bc; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int k_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; k_s[c * k_pitch + d] = k[k_idx]; } __syncthreads(); for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += float(q_s[r * q_pitch + d]) * float(k_s[c * k_pitch + d]); } s_s[r * s_pitch + c] = sum * scale; } __syncthreads(); for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { float row_max = -INFINITY; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { row_max = fmaxf(row_max, s_s[r * s_pitch + c]); } float m_prev = m[r]; float m_new = fmaxf(m_prev, row_max); float alpha = expf(m_prev - m_new); float row_sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { row_sum += expf(s_s[r * s_pitch + c] - m_new); } l[r] = alpha * l[r] + row_sum; m[r] = m_new; } __syncthreads(); } // Pass 2 for (int j_tile = 0; j_tile < num_j_tiles; ++j_tile) { int j_start = j_tile * Bc; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int k_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; k_s[c * k_pitch + d] = k[k_idx]; int v_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; v_s[c * v_pitch + d] = v[v_idx]; } __syncthreads(); for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += float(q_s[r * q_pitch + d]) * float(k_s[c * k_pitch + d]); } s_s[r * s_pitch + c] = sum * scale; } __syncthreads(); for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float p_val = expf(s_s[r * s_pitch + c] - m[r]) / l[r]; s_s[r * s_pitch + c] = p_val; int p_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * N + j_start + c; p[p_idx] = at::BFloat16(p_val); } __syncthreads(); for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; float sum = o_acc[r * o_pitch + d]; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { sum += s_s[r * s_pitch + c] * float(v_s[c * v_pitch + d]); } o_acc[r * o_pitch + d] = sum; } __syncthreads(); } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int o_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; o[o_idx] = at::BFloat16(o_acc[r * o_pitch + d]); } } template <int Br, int D> __global__ void compute_d_kernel( const at::BFloat16* o, const at::BFloat16* dout, float* __restrict__ d_vec, int B, int Hq, int N) { const int o_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); int b = blockIdx.z / Hq; int hq = blockIdx.z % Hq; int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int i_start = i_tile * Br; extern __shared__ char smem[]; at::BFloat16* o_s = (at::BFloat16*)smem; at::BFloat16* dO_s = o_s + Br * o_pitch; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int o_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; o_s[r * o_pitch + d] = o[o_idx]; dO_s[r * o_pitch + d] = dout[o_idx]; } __syncthreads(); for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { float sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += float(o_s[r * o_pitch + d]) * float(dO_s[r * o_pitch + d]); } d_vec[(b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r] = sum; } } template <int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void compute_ds_kernel( const at::BFloat16* dout, const at::BFloat16* v, const float* d_vec, const at::BFloat16* p, at::BFloat16* __restrict__ ds, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N) { const int dO_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); int i_start = i_tile * Br; int j_start = j_tile * Bc; extern __shared__ char smem[]; at::BFloat16* dO_s = (at::BFloat16*)smem; at::BFloat16* v_s = dO_s + Br * dO_pitch; at::BFloat16* p_s = v_s + Bc * v_pitch; float* d_s = (float*)(p_s + Br * p_smem_pitch); for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int dO_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; dO_s[r * dO_pitch + d] = dout[dO_idx]; } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int v_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; v_s[c * v_pitch + d] = v[v_idx]; } for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { d_s[r] = d_vec[(b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r]; } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; int p_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * N + j_start + c; p_s[r * p_smem_pitch + c] = p[p_idx]; } __syncthreads(); for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; float dp = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { dp += float(dO_s[r * dO_pitch + d]) * float(v_s[c * v_pitch + d]); } float p_val = float(p_s[r * p_smem_pitch + c]); float ds_val = (dp - d_s[r]) * p_val; int ds_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * N + j_start + c; ds[ds_idx] = at::BFloat16(ds_val); } } template <int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void gemm_dq_kernel( const at::BFloat16* ds, const at::BFloat16* k, at::BFloat16* __restrict__ dq, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, float scale) { const int ds_pitch = Bc + 1; const int k_pitch = PITCH_F(D); const int dq_pitch = PITCH_F(D); int b = blockIdx.z / Hq; int hq = blockIdx.z % Hq; int kv = hq / (Hq / Hkv); int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int i_start = i_tile * Br; int num_j_tiles = N / Bc; extern __shared__ char smem[]; float* ds_s = (float*)smem; float* k_s = (float*)(ds_s + Br * ds_pitch); float* dq_acc = (float*)(k_s + Bc * k_pitch); for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { dq_acc[idx] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); for (int j_tile = 0; j_tile < num_j_tiles; ++j_tile) { int j_start = j_tile * Bc; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * Bc; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / Bc; int c = idx % Bc; int ds_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * N + j_start + c; ds_s[r * ds_pitch + c] = float(ds[ds_idx]); } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Bc * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int c = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int k_idx = ((b * Hkv + kv) * N + j_start + c) * D + d; k_s[c * k_pitch + d] = float(k[k_idx]); } __syncthreads(); for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; const int q_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); const int dk_pitch = PITCH_F(D); int b = blockIdx.z / Hkv; int kv = blockIdx.z % Hkv; int j_tile = blockIdx.x; int j_start = j_tile * Bc; int num_q_per_kv = Hq / Hkv; dk[dk_idx] = at::BFloat16(dk_acc[c * dk_pitch + d] * scale); } } template <int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void gemm_dv_kernel( const at::BFloat16* p, const at::BFloat16* dout, at::BFloat16* __restrict__ dv, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N) { const int p_pitch = PITCH_BF16(Bc); dv[dv_idx] = at::BFloat16(dv_acc[c * dv_pitch + d]); } } std::vector<torch::Tensor> attention_backward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v, torch::Tensor dout) { int B = q.size(0); int Hq = q.size(1); int N = q.size(2); int D = q.size(3); int Hkv = k.size(1); auto opts = q.options(); torch::Tensor p = torch::empty({B, Hq, N, N}, opts); torch::Tensor o = torch::empty({B, Hq, N, D}, opts); torch::Tensor d_vec = torch::empty({B, Hq, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kFloat32).device(q.device())); But before that, I want to consider if there's any chance of numerical mismatch. For example, in our softmax forward, we compute `P = exp(S - m) / l`. This is standard. The reference `F.softmax` computes the same. There could be tiny differences in the order of operations: we compute max and sum with a specific order (loop over j_tiles). For a given row, the online algorithm computes the max of all elements exactly as `max` over all j_tiles, and the sum as `sum(exp(S - m))`. This is mathematically identical to standard softmax. The difference is only floating point rounding. Since we use float for m and l, and bf16 for inputs, the result should be close. In the backward, we compute `dS = (dP - D) * P`. Standard formula. `dP = dO @ V^T`. Our dot product uses float accumulation. PyTorch matmul also uses float accumulation (on Tensor Cores). The order of summation differs. For tolerance 1e-2 in bf16, it's fine. Let's write the code now. I will produce a single code block with the complete Python code. Actually, the latest PyTorch `load_inline` signature: `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline(name, cpp_sources, cuda_sources=None, functions=None, extra_cuda_cflags=None, extra_cflags=None, verbose=False, ...)` We can do: ```python module = load_inline( name="custom_gqa_bwd_hopper", cpp_sources="", cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE, functions=["attention_backward"], extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-std=c++17"], verbose=False, ) ``` Sometimes `cpp_sources=""` can cause issues. If so, we can put a dummy C++ source or just include everything in `cuda_sources`. But `load_inline` compiles `cpp_sources` as `.cpp` and `cuda_sources` as `.cu`. So both can be provided. assistant```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline CUDA_SOURCE = r""" #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <math.h> #define PITCH_BF16(D) ((D) + 2) #define PITCH_F(D) ((D) + 1) template <int Br, int Bc, int D> __global__ void compute_p_and_o_kernel( const at::BFloat16* q, const at::BFloat16* k, const at::BFloat16* v, at::BFloat16* __restrict__ p, at::BFloat16* __restrict__ o, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, float scale) { const int q_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); int i_tile = blockIdx.x; int i_start = i_tile * Br; int num_j_tiles = N / Bc; extern __shared__ char smem[]; at::BFloat16* q_s = (at::BFloat16*)smem; at::BFloat16* k_s = q_s + Br * q_pitch; at::BFloat16* v_s = k_s + Bc * k_pitch; float* s_s = (float*)(v_s + Bc * v_pitch); float* o_acc = s_s + Br * s_pitch; float* m = o_acc + Br * o_pitch; float* l = m + Br; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int q_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; q_s[r * q_pitch + d] = q[q_idx]; } if (threadIdx.x < Br) { int r = threadIdx.x; m[r] = -INFINITY; l[r] = 0.0f; } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { o_acc[idx] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); // Pass 1: row-wise softmax statistics for (int j_tile = 0; j_tile < num_j_tiles; ++j_tile) { int j_start = j_tile * Bc; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; } s_s[r * s_pitch + c] = sum * scale; } __syncthreads(); for (int r = threadIdx.x; r < Br; r += blockDim.x) { float row_max = -INFINITY; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { row_max = fmaxf(row_max, s_s[r * s_pitch + c]); } float m_prev = m[r]; float m_new = fmaxf(m_prev, row_max); float alpha = expf(m_prev - m_new); float row_sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < Bc; ++c) { row_sum += expf(s_s[r * s_pitch + c] - m_new); } l[r] = alpha * l[r] + row_sum; m[r] = m_new; } __syncthreads(); } // Pass 2: write P and accumulate O for (int j_tile = 0; j_tile < num_j_tiles; ++j_tile) { int j_start = j_tile * Bc; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; } o_acc[r * o_pitch + d] = sum; } __syncthreads(); } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < Br * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int r = idx / D; int d = idx % D; int o_idx = ((b * Hq + hq) * N + i_start + r) * D + d; o[o_idx] = at::BFloat16(o_acc[r * o_pitch + d]); } } template <int Br, int D> __global__ void compute_d_kernel( const at::BFloat16* o, const at::BFloat16* dout, float* __restrict__ d_vec, int B, int Hq, int N) { const int o_pitch = PITCH_BF16(D); reasoning

Since D=128 in this case, I need to either specialize the templates for D=128 or pass D as a runtime parameter and handle it differently. Let me restructure the kernels to handle this properly. reasoning
The issue is that we're providing both cpp_sources and cuda_sources, but both contain PYBIND11_MODULE. We should only include it in the cpp_sources, not in the CUDA source. Let me remove the PYBIND11_MODULE from the CUDA source.
